| D-042 | 2026-05-23 | An Administrator creates a new Control Panel user from the CP via `POST /api/v1/admin/users` (page `/admin/users/new`). The new account is created in the `Approved` state with **no password**; the API mints a `PasswordReset`-purpose `AccountCode` with a **7-day lifetime** and emails the new user an invitation containing the 6-digit code. The user follows the link to `/reset-password`, enters email + code + new password, and signs in normally. The actor optionally toggles a "Grant Administrator role" checkbox; no other role is grantable today (the wider role catalogue waits for gate D1 / CPD-001 OI-3). A duplicate email returns 409 `ADMIN_EMAIL_ALREADY_REGISTERED`. Every creation audits `Admin.UserCreated` with both `ActorUserId` (the admin) and `SubjectUserId` (the new user). Companion `GET /api/v1/admin/users` returns every account (no paging — bounded user count) with email, display name, lifecycle state, 2FA flag, role flag and creation date; rendered at `/admin/users` with a per-row "Reset 2FA" link that deep-links to `/admin/reset-2fa?email=…`. Both pages are gated by `[Authorize(Roles = "Administrator")]` and a CP-side proxy keeps the access token server-only (D-037). | First slice of `myComment` #33 (User Management). Reuses the existing password-reset rail — no new code-issuance machinery, no new email template variant, no new endpoint on the user side. The 7-day invite lifetime matches GitHub / Microsoft 365 invite codes (the self-service forgot-password 10-min code stays short because the user is right there clicking). Admin never picks the password — invite-link is the OWASP / NIST 800-63B baseline for admin-issued accounts (the admin is never exposed to a plaintext password). The list page is intentionally minimal today (no search / filter / paging) — adding it costs little and removes the "how do I find the email for /admin/reset-2fa" friction that would otherwise force the admin to consult the DB. The deferred operations (approve pending visitor, soft-delete, role-change beyond Administrator) wait for the larger module increment that ships once gate D1 lands. Five integration tests pin the contract; the invite-flow test reaches end-to-end (admin creates → DB code → user resets password → user signs in). |
| D-041 | 2026-05-23 | Recovery from a lost authenticator + lost recovery codes is **admin-driven**, not self-service email. A new `POST /api/v1/admin/users/reset-two-factor` endpoint, gated on the `Administrator` role, wipes the target's authenticator key, recovery codes, `TwoFactorEnabled` flag, security stamp and refresh tokens, and queues a notification email to the target. The actor cannot reset themselves (use the profile-page Disable instead) and cannot reset another `Administrator` (separation of privileges — the super-administrator's recovery path stays out-of-band via the seeder + `appsettings.json` re-pair). A mandatory free-text reason (10–500 chars) is captured in the audit row alongside both actor and subject user ids (new `OperationLog.ActorUserId` column). The CP page is `/admin/reset-2fa` with a `[Authorize(Roles = "Administrator")]` gate, a confirmation dialog, and bilingual strings. Operator-level SQL reset stays documented as the fallback for the super-administrator (see `docs/SIMF-OPS-001-Recovery-Procedures.md`). Email-as-self-service recovery (the alternative considered) was **rejected** for SIMF's defence / MoD-confidential context: email isn't a strong second factor, and treating an email account as an MFA bypass is policy-wrong for any role-holder. Visitor self-recovery isn't needed today because visitors don't have 2FA opt-in. | Mirrors the enterprise SSO baseline (Okta / Azure AD admin-driven reset). The new `ActorUserId` field is the cleanest seam for future privileged actions (account approve / delete, role assignment) and is nullable so every existing audit row stays valid. The Administrator-vs-Administrator block is the standard "no peer demotion" rule — it stops a compromised admin from neutralising the rest of the admin tier. Notification email is the out-of-band signal that defeats silent abuse. The reset uses the same internal wipe `TotpEnrollmentService.DisableAsync` already does, plus the recovery-code revoke + refresh-token revoke + security-stamp roll, all in one transaction. Six integration tests pin the contract; an admin-reset failure (self-reset, admin-vs-admin, missing target) emits `Admin.TwoFactorResetFailed` with the error code so the SIEM can alert on abuse attempts. |
| D-040 | 2026-05-23 | TOTP enrolment issues **ten single-use recovery codes** as a lost-authenticator fallback. Codes are 10-character Crockford-base32 (no `0/O/1/I/L/U`) printed as `XXXXX-XXXXX`; ~49 bits of entropy each; stored as SHA-256 hex hashes in a new `TotpRecoveryCodes` table; revealed plaintext exactly once in the `TotpConfirmResponse` (and in `RecoveryCodesResponse` on regenerate) and never again. A user signs in with a recovery code at the new `POST /api/v1/auth/verify-recovery-code` endpoint using the same MFA-token ticket the TOTP step uses — a recovery code is an *alternative* second factor, not a 2FA bypass. Regenerating wipes the previous batch atomically; disabling 2FA wipes the codes too. **Same sign-in flow choice update**: the second-factor kind is now TOTP for **any user with an authenticator key**, not only role-holders, so a visitor who enrols in 2FA from the profile page actually signs in via TOTP (and can also recover via recovery code). The visitor email-OTP path is preserved for users with no authenticator key. Wrong recovery-code attempts call `AccessFailedAsync` (same lockout budget as a wrong TOTP), and the existing ticket attempt-count gates brute force per session. Audit events: `Totp.RecoveryCodesGenerated`, `Totp.RecoveryCodesRegenerated`, `Totp.RecoveryCodeUsed`, `Totp.RecoveryCodeFailed`. Profile page surfaces "X of 10 remaining" with a "Regenerate" button and a low-codes warning when ≤ 3 remain. | OWASP / GitHub / Microsoft / Google industry baseline. The lost-authenticator gap had no in-app recovery before this — operators were left with direct SQL or a re-seed for the super-admin (see "How to recover" investigation 2026-05-23). The Crockford alphabet matches what makes a code easy to read off paper. Hashing with SHA-256 (not Argon2) is the same scheme `RefreshToken` and `SecondFactorToken` use — high-entropy tokens don't need a slow KDF. Decision to base the second-factor kind on the authenticator-key presence (not just role) is small in code but materially correct: a visitor who has opted into TOTP must sign in via TOTP, otherwise the recovery flow would be unreachable for them. The legacy role-only path stays as a fallback for any pre-enrolment user. Existing visitor integration tests opt in by calling `EnableTwoFactor`, which sets only `TwoFactorEnabled` (no authenticator key) — those still go through email-OTP, so the change is back-compatible. |
| D-039 | 2026-05-23 | User avatars move from `varbinary(max)` in the user row to the filesystem under a configured `Storage:AvatarBase` directory, mirroring the IBS V10 `UploadCarImagesEndpoint` convention. `SimfUser.Avatar` + `SimfUser.AvatarContentType` are dropped; `SimfUser.AvatarRelativePath` (`nvarchar(260)`) replaces them. The API exposes an authenticated streamer `GET /api/v1/account/avatar/{userId:guid}` that resolves the user's row, opens the file via `IAvatarStorage`, and streams it back with the right `Content-Type` and `Cache-Control: private, max-age=300`. The CP proxies that endpoint at `GET /account/api/avatar/{userId}`, so the browser fetches same-origin (no CORS). `ProfileResponse.AvatarUrl` / `AvatarResponse.AvatarUrl` now carry the CP-relative URL with a `?v={UpdatedAt.UtcTicks}` cache-buster — a fresh upload always defeats the browser cache. This **supersedes D-035** (DB-bytes). | The DB-bytes approach (D-035) inflated every profile fetch by 33 % (base64 in JSON) and made the top-bar avatar pre-fetch a multi-megabyte payload per circuit boot. The IBS pattern is the project-wide convention for user-supplied images and is what STARTIME has running in production. The cutover follows IBS exactly: filename is `{userId:N}.{ext}` (extension derived from the validated content type, never from the client's `FileName`); atomic write via temp-file + move; previous files for the same user are deleted before the new file is written, so an extension change leaves no orphan; the IBS magic-byte sniff (JPEG `FF D8 FF`, PNG `89 50 4E 47 0D 0A 1A 0A`, RIFF/WEBP) stays. The existing in-DB avatar (super-admin's one uploaded image during the e2e) is **not** backfilled — the migration drops the bytes; one user simply re-uploads after deploy. Production deployment must set `Storage:AvatarBase` via env var; the API fails fast at boot if unset. |
| D-038 | 2026-05-23 | The five-agent review's SEV-1 / SEV-2 findings on Part B Stage 2 are addressed in the same commit; the lower-severity follow-ups (out-of-band 2FA-disable email, TimeProvider-driven enrolment tests, CP proxy bUnit / integration tests, per-user rate-limit partitioning, hidden-secret toggle, magic-byte-decoded image canonicalisation, refresh-token revoke on TOTP disable) are tracked in MEMORY for later increments. **Fixes applied this commit:** (1) Profile change-password now signs out via a hidden POST form (`Nav.NavigateTo("/auth/sign-out")` was a GET to a POST-only route — the cookie was staying alive); (2) the file `<input>`'s `disabled` attribute uses `null`-omission instead of `"@_busy"` (which rendered `disabled="False"` and locked the input even when idle); (3) avatar success messages render inside the avatar section, not the 2FA section (separate `_avatarOk` field); (4) the CP `/account/api/…` proxy forwards the **upstream HTTP status** verbatim (401 / 423 / 429 / 503) via a new `ApiCallResult<T>` wrapper, instead of collapsing every failure to 400; (5) `ProfileEndpoint` and `AvatarDeleteEndpoint` join the "auth" rate-limit policy alongside the other authenticated endpoints; (6) `TotpEnrollmentService.Confirm` and `Disable` call `userManager.AccessFailedAsync` on a wrong code so brute-force is bounded by the account-lockout budget, mirroring `SignInService`; (7) `AccountService.SetAvatarAsync` validates the image **magic bytes** against the declared MIME (PNG / JPEG / WebP signatures), so a polyglot or mislabelled payload is rejected before it touches the user row; (8) avatar rejections (size, MIME, missing file) now emit `Avatar.Rejected` audit entries with the rejection's error code; (9) the successful-upload audit detail now carries `mime=…;size=…` for SIEM hunting; (10) new tests pin the empty-file path, the case-insensitive MIME, the magic-byte mismatch, avatar replacement, the disable-then-re-enrol cycle, and the non-empty `MessageArabic` invariant on every error. | Direct response to the engineering-code-reviewer (SEV-1.1, SEV-2.4), engineering-security-engineer (SEV-1.2, SEV-1.3, SEV-2.1), engineering-threat-detection-engineer (S1-1, S1-2, S1-4, S1-5), testing-reality-checker (the GET-to-POST and `disabled="@_busy"` bugs), and testing-test-results-analyzer (SEV-1 coverage gaps) findings recorded against the working tree on top of commit `b536461`. The Arabic test pins D-030 in the test layer too. |
| D-037 | 2026-05-23 | The Control Panel `/account/profile` page calls the API through a **CP-side proxy endpoint group** under `/account/api/…` rather than calling the SIMF API directly from the Blazor circuit. The browser sends the auth cookie via `fetch` (same origin); the proxy endpoint reads the access token from the cookie's stored auth tokens and forwards the call to the API. | Blazor Server with global `InteractiveServerNoPrerender` doesn't expose `HttpContext` to interactive event handlers, so the access token cannot be read from the circuit. Routing through a CP-side endpoint keeps the access token entirely server-side (the Blazor page never sees it), is the same pattern as the existing `/auth/sign-out` form-post, and works uniformly for the four profile sections (2FA enrol, change password, roles, avatar). Long-term, a circuit-scoped `IAccessTokenSource` populated via a `CircuitHandler` would let the page call the API directly; that refactor is recorded as a follow-up. |
| D-036 | 2026-05-23 | Authenticator-app enrolment uses a **two-slot pattern**: a freshly issued secret is stashed under the SIMF-owned provider token `[SIMF]/PendingAuthenticatorKey` and only promoted to ASP.NET Core Identity's active slot `[AspNetUserStore]/AuthenticatorKey` (and `TwoFactorEnabled = true`) once the user proves the QR was paired by submitting a first valid code. Disable removes the active slot and clears `LastUsedTotpTimestep` so a re-enrol starts from a clean replay guard. The QR is rendered server-side as SVG via QRCoder. | The active TOTP slot must be the one the sign-in step verifies; a never-confirmed key in the active slot would lock a user out at the next sign-in. The pending slot keeps the candidate secret on the user row (atomic with the account) so the user can complete enrolment across page reloads. Server-side SVG QR avoids a client-side QR JavaScript dependency, keeps the secret never-rendered in plain HTML outside the QR image bytes, and respects the brand colours (navy on white). |
| D-035 | 2026-05-23 | User avatars are stored as `varbinary(max)` on `SimfUser` (the `Avatar` and `AvatarContentType` columns), not on the filesystem. The API returns the avatar as a base64 `data:` URI inside the profile JSON. Accepted MIME types: `image/png`, `image/jpeg`, `image/webp`; size cap 2 MB. | Single source of truth — the avatar is atomic with the user row, with no separate path or permission to manage. The data-URI return removes the cross-origin static-file fetch the CP browser would otherwise have to make against the API host, and removes the need for a separate authenticated `GET avatar` endpoint. For SIMF's bounded user count (event attendance), the row-size trade-off is acceptable. A future move to object storage / a CDN remains a single-column change behind the same contract. |
| D-034 | 2026-05-23 | `TotpVerifier` normalises a TOTP secret before Base32 decoding — strips whitespace and uppercases — so a key with the spaces an authenticator app shows (e.g. `"abcd efgh ijkl …"`) decodes correctly. | Diagnosing myComment #35: the seeded super-admin key `dbji csx7 c3mj s2qa sjcl rbcl kiqk ovr3` made `Base32Encoding.ToBytes` throw on the space character; the verifier caught the exception and silently fail-closed, so every code looked wrong. The fix matches what Google Authenticator does when you paste a spaced key. The diagnosis was that the time-source is UTC and correct (Otp.NET uses `DateTimeOffset.UtcNow`); the secret format was the only issue. |
| D-033 | 2026-05-23 | The sign-in second factor is skipped when the account has `TwoFactorEnabled = false`. The API issues tokens directly on the password step and `SignInResponse.Tokens` carries them; `MfaRequired` is `false`, `MfaToken` and `OtpToken` are null. This applies to **both** Control Panel users (today: TOTP) and Website visitors (today: email OTP). | myComment item #34 explicitly mandated the behaviour for both apps ("for 2FA disabled yes in both"). The contract change is small — one new optional field on `SignInResponse` — and the existing OTP/TOTP flows are unchanged when 2FA is on. The CP and Website sign-in pages branch on the response: tokens present → complete sign-in; otherwise → continue to the second-factor page. The existing integration tests opt accounts into 2FA so they keep exercising the OTP and TOTP paths; three new tests cover the 2FA-off path. |
| D-032 | 2026-05-23 | The brand panel renders the **SIMF logo** at the top and the **SIMF wordmark text** directly below it (reversed, white-on-navy), then the subtitle and caption. This reverses D-025, which removed the wordmark when the white logo was placed. | myComment / owner feedback on the login screen: "make logo at top, then back SIMF txt". The wordmark was removed in D-025 on the assumption the logo replaced it; the owner wanted them stacked (insignia → service name), which matches typical navy / government brand convention. |
| D-031 | 2026-05-23 | The Control Panel and Website login pages are fully localised (English / Arabic) **now**, with an AR/EN language switch on each page's top-control row. This reverses D-022, which deferred login-page localisation to a later increment. | myComment item #14 made multi-language mandatory, and the owner pointed out that the login page — the first page a user sees — is monolingual with no language switch. D-022 was deferring the wrong thing. The Website also gains its own localisation infrastructure (`AddLocalization`, `UseRequestLocalization`, a `/culture` endpoint and `Strings.{en,ar}.resx`) — it had none before. The brand panel's subtitle / caption come from the resource files too, so the navy panel reads `"Control Panel" / "لوحة التحكم"` in lock-step with the rest of the page. |
| D-030 | 2026-05-23 | Every SIMF API error carries the message in **both** English and Arabic. `ApiError`, `ApiErrorDetail` and `ApiException` add a `required` `MessageArabic` field. FluentValidation rules attach the Arabic message as their `CustomState`; the response builder pairs them on the way out. The Blazor pages pick the language with `MessageForCurrentCulture()`. This **reverses** the SIMF-API-001 §7 stance of one-language-per-Accept-Language. | The owner mandated multilingual error messages (myComment item #14: "Apply multi language in error, this mandatory"). Returning both languages on every response — instead of negotiating one per request — lets a single API call serve any client culture, removes the cross-call language drift risk during a language switch, and matches the controlled-document one-pass change the project's customer asked for. The Identity-error descriptions raised by ASP.NET Core Identity itself are mapped to Arabic by `IdentityErrorTranslator` keyed on the Identity error code. The controlled document (SIMF-API-001 §7) will be updated to reflect this in its next revision. |
| D-029 | 2026-05-22 | The Control Panel is default-deny — `[Authorize]` in `Components/_Imports.razor` requires a session for every page. The anonymous surface is: the sign-in-flow pages (sign-in, the TOTP step, forgot-password, reset-password), the framework error and not-found pages, and the `/auth/complete` and `/culture` endpoints. | Global CLAUDE.md §4 allows anonymous access only for SignIn / SignUp / ForgotPassword. The Control Panel's sign-in is a multi-step flow (password → TOTP → cookie completion); every step, plus reset-password and the error/not-found pages, must be reachable without a session. `/auth/complete` issues the authentication cookie (it runs before the cookie exists) and `/culture` sets the language cookie — both are necessarily pre- or cross-session. This broader-but-equivalent anonymous set is recorded here per §18; everything else is protected by the `_Imports` default. The `/auth/sign-out` endpoint requires authentication, which — with the SameSite=Lax cookie — also defeats cross-site forced sign-out. |
| D-028 | 2026-05-22 | The Control Panel's default interface language is English; Arabic is fully supported and switched from the top bar. The shell, navigation and dashboard are localised; the Control Panel's own sign-in pages remain English for now. | SIMF-CPD-001 §10 makes Arabic the primary language. The localisation machinery — resource files, the culture cookie, RTL through CSS logical properties — supports both languages fully, and the default is a one-line configuration value. English is kept as the default for this increment so the already-built English sign-in pages render correctly; making Arabic the default and localising the sign-in pages is a small, bounded follow-up for the owner to confirm. |
| D-027 | 2026-05-22 | The Control Panel base shell — top bar, nine-group side navigation, content region — is built from new `Simf*` components (`SimfAppShell`, `SimfNavGroup`, `SimfNavItem`, `SimfPageHeader`) and is fully bilingual (English / Arabic, LTR / RTL). Permission filtering of the navigation is **not** applied. | The shell follows SIMF-CPD-001 §6. Per SIMF-CPD-001 §7 the navigation is permission-filtered, which depends on the per-type permission map — gate D1 / SIMF-CPD-001 OI-3 — not yet available; until it lands the navigation shows its full structure. The Control Panel's render mode is set globally (Interactive Server, no prerender) because a page-level render mode does not extend to the page's layout, and the shell layout must be interactive. |
| D-026 | 2026-05-22 | The Control Panel issues a persistent authentication cookie on sign-in. Because a cookie can only be written in an HTTP request context, the interactive verification page stashes the completed token pair in a short-lived, single-use, server-side ticket and full-page-navigates to a completion endpoint that issues the cookie. | A Blazor interactive circuit cannot write a cookie — the response has already begun. The ticket hand-off keeps the interactive verification experience while moving the cookie write to an HTTP endpoint. The cookie carries the user identity and, encrypted, the SIMF API tokens for the module pages that will call the API; access-token refresh on expiry is a later concern. |
| D-025 | 2026-05-22 | The white (negative) SIMF logo, delivered by the owner, is placed on the navy brand panel of the sign-in screens (`SimfBrandPanel`). The white auth card keeps the `SimfWordmark` text placeholder. | The owner supplied the negative logo — the correct version for the dark brand panel (SIMF-VID-001 §5.2). It was delivered as a PNG (864×258), not the SVG SIMF-VID-001 §9.3 asks for, and only the negative version is in hand; the standard (dark) logo for the white card, and SVG forms of both, remain outstanding under SIMF-VID-001 OI-2. The PNG is stored as an RCL static asset (`_content/SIMF.Components/img/simf-logo.png`) and swaps for the SVG with no layout change when it arrives. |
| D-024 | 2026-05-22 | The public Website's content is the pre-built static SIMF 2026 marketing site (`index.html` + `content.js` + `assets/`), imported **verbatim** into `SIMF.Web/wwwroot` and served at `/`. The Blazor signed-in landing placeholder moved from `/` to `/account`. | The owner supplied the finished marketing site (`simf-2026.pages.dev`) and instructed it be added unchanged — no rewrite. A static site is served as-is by the static-file middleware (`UseDefaultFiles` + `UseStaticFiles`); porting it to Razor would be a rewrite. The Blazor authentication pages keep their own routes (`/login`, `/forgot-password`, …) and the post-sign-in landing moved to `/account` so `/` is free for the marketing site. The site keeps its own logo and assets and the FS Albert Arabic + Google-Fonts files it shipped with. About 19 MB of binary assets (a hero video, images, fonts, a 5.3 MB `content.js`) were added to the repository as a result. The provided `logo.svg` file was not used — the imported site keeps its own logo. |
| D-023 | 2026-05-22 | Automated tests for the frontend login increment cover the logic-bearing code — `SimfAuthClient` and `SimfAuthSession` — with xUnit (`tests/SIMF.ApiClient.Tests`). The `Simf*` components and the authentication pages are verified by running the apps and capturing screenshots (light and dark themes, the field-validation state). | The API client (envelope handling, transport-failure mapping) and the session holder (`MaskEmail`, state transitions) carry real logic and are unit-tested. The components are largely declarative markup; rendering tests with bUnit are a tracked follow-up rather than a blocker for a first frontend increment, and the visual verification is the recorded evidence in the meantime. |
| D-022 | 2026-05-22 | The authentication pages ship with English interface text. Full Arabic/English resource-file localisation (SIMF-CPD-001 §10) is delivered with the Control Panel base-shell increment. | The `Simf*` components are built RTL-ready — CSS logical properties throughout — and the `SimfLanguageSwitch` component exists; only the resource files and the culture-cookie wiring are deferred. SIMF-CPD-001 §10 frames localisation as a shell-level concern (the language switch lives in the top bar, remembered per user), so it is delivered once, with the shell, rather than half-built for the login pages and redone. This matches the agreed frontend-increment scope. |
| D-021 | 2026-05-22 | The `Simf*` login components are built as semantic HTML styled by `theme.tokens.css`, not as MudBlazor wrappers. | The approved 2026-05-20 design decision makes `Simf*` the UI vocabulary and explicitly overrides the earlier "MudBlazor only" recommendation. Plain HTML + token CSS renders identically under server-side rendering and Interactive Server, needs no JavaScript for the form tier, and gives full control of the brand and of RTL. MudBlazor stays mandated for the Control Panel's rich interactive widgets — data tables, dialogs, navigation — and is introduced with the CP base-shell increment. |
| D-020 | 2026-05-22 | `theme.tokens.css` carries a proposed navy-derived dark-theme token set (a `[data-theme="dark"]` block) so the Control Panel and Website ship a working dark theme now. | SIMF-CPD-001 §8.5 / OI-1 leaves the dark-theme token values to be reviewed. The values here are **proposed for client review**, consistent with how SIMF-CPD-001 §8.4 already treats the derived functional tokens. Only token values change between themes; no component or page is rewritten, so a reviewed change is a single-file edit. |
| D-019 | 2026-05-22 | Frontend project layout: `src/ControlPanel/SIMF.ControlPanel` (the CP app), `src/Website/SIMF.Web` (the Website), `src/Shared/SIMF.Components` (the `Simf*` Razor component library), `src/Shared/SIMF.ApiClient` (the typed Login-API client), `tests/SIMF.ApiClient.Tests`. | SIMF-SES-001 §4.1 fixes a `/src/ControlPanel` folder and the top-level layout but does not name the projects and gives the Website no folder. The component prefix is `Simf` — matching the SIMF acronym and every existing `SIMF.*` project; the 2026-05-20 design-decision note recorded it as `Smif*`, a typo, corrected here. |
| D-018 | 2026-05-22 | The public Website ships a sign-in / forgot-password / reset-password flow now, ahead of SIMF-FDS-001 OI-3. | OI-3 records "whether the website offers sign-in" as still to be confirmed with the client; the owner instructed the Website login be built. Recorded so OI-3 is closed by a deliberate decision, not by omission. If the client decides the Website offers no sign-in, the Website authentication pages are removed — the `Simf*` library and the Control Panel are unaffected. |
| D-017 | 2026-05-22 | The Control Panel is a Blazor Server application (Interactive Server render mode). The public Website is server-side rendered by default, with the authentication pages opting into Interactive Server. | SIMF-CPD-001 §4 mandates Blazor + MudBlazor for the Control Panel but leaves the render flavour unspecified; the owner chose Interactive Server. It keeps the JWT on the server (the token never reaches the browser, and the API call is server-to-server with no cross-origin concern) and matches MudBlazor's interactivity model. The Website is SSR for its future public content; the authentication pages need interactivity — a submitting state, inline validation, show/hide password — so they are Interactive Server islands on an otherwise SSR site. |
| D-016 | 2026-05-22 | **Deferred hardening.** `AccountCode` rows (the six-digit email-verification, sign-in-OTP and password-reset codes) are stored in plaintext. Hashing the codes at rest is recorded as a pre-freeze hardening item. | The codes are short-lived (10 minutes for password reset), single-use (`ConsumedAt` is set on first valid use), and attempt-capped (5 wrong tries per code). A database-read attacker who already has the `AccountCode` table also has the `Users` table and the security stamps, so plaintext codes are not the weakest link for that threat. Hashing them (same SHA-256 pattern as `OpaqueToken`) is still worth doing before freeze for defence-in-depth and is logged here so it is not lost. |
| D-015 | 2026-05-22 | **Accepted risk.** The password-reset six-digit code has a bounded brute-force surface — a per-code attempt cap of 5 and a per-account re-issue cap of 5 per hour — giving at most about 25 guesses per hour against a 10⁶ code space. This is accepted rather than further tightened. | Consistent with the sign-in-OTP bound already accepted in D-008 and D-011. About 25 guesses/hour against a six-digit code is a ~1-in-40 000 chance per hour; combined with the 10-minute code lifetime and per-IP rate limiting the residual risk is acceptable for the Login API. Revisit if the reset model changes. |
| D-014 | 2026-05-22 | Reset-password sets the new password with `RemovePasswordAsync` + `AddPasswordAsync` inside a transaction, rather than ASP.NET Identity's `GeneratePasswordResetTokenAsync` / `ResetPasswordAsync`. | The Identity reset-token provider (`AddDefaultTokenProviders`) is not available in the Infrastructure class library — it ships with the ASP.NET Core shared framework, not the `Microsoft.Extensions.Identity.*` packages. SIMF's own six-digit `PasswordReset` code is the authorisation; remove-then-add still runs the Identity password validators and rolls the security stamp, and the transaction keeps the pair atomic so a rejected password leaves the old one intact. (TOTP verification likewise uses Otp.NET for the same packaging reason — increment 4b.) |
| D-013 | 2026-05-22 | The `OnTokenValidated` security-stamp check does one database read per authenticated request. Refresh-token row pruning and an `OperationLog` severity column are deferred to a later operations task. | The per-request stamp read is the cost of instant access-token revocation on sign-out; with few protected endpoints today it is acceptable. At Control Panel scale it is revisited with a short-TTL cache or the ASP.NET Identity `SecurityStampValidator` interval. Revoked/expired refresh-token rows accumulate (kept for the reuse-detection forensic trail) — a retention prune and a `Severity` column for SIEM alerting are an operations-hardening item, recorded so they are not lost. |
| D-012 | 2026-05-22 | Sign-out ends **every** session for the account — it revokes all of the account's refresh tokens and rolls the security stamp — rather than only the current session. | The access token is stateless and cannot be revoked individually; the security stamp is the revocation signal and is per-account, so rolling it necessarily ends every session. Revoking all refresh tokens as well keeps the two consistent. A per-session sign-out would need a session identifier in the token and is not in the Login API scope. |
| D-011 | 2026-05-22 | The sign-in second-factor ticket and the emailed OTP code are correlated by user id and "latest unconsumed", not bound by a foreign key. | The increment-4b security review suggested an FK from the OTP code to the ticket. The OTP brute-force surface is instead bounded by two enforced caps — the per-ticket attempt cap (5) and the per-account sign-in-OTP re-issue cap (5 per hour) — together limiting guessing to about 25 codes/hour against a six-digit code, independent of the binding. The FK is a structural-clarity improvement, deferred; revisit if the sign-in OTP model grows. |
| D-010 | 2026-05-21 | Sign-in succeeds for an account in `EmailVerified`, `PendingApproval` or `Approved` state; it is blocked for `Registered` (email not verified) and for `Disabled` / `Rejected`. The access a not-yet-`Approved` user then has is an authorisation concern, not a sign-in block. | SIMF-RPM-001 §10 states a `PendingApproval` visitor signs in and sees a limited status view. SIMF-API-001 OI-4 (gate D1) is the authoritative open item on not-yet-approved access; this is the sign-in-layer interpretation pending its closure. |
| D-009 | 2026-05-21 | The operation log is written to the application database with a Serilog file sink; a SIEM / central-log integration, alerting rules, a retention policy and append-only (tamper-evident) enforcement are **not** part of increment 4a — they are scheduled as a separate monitoring task before go-live. | Increment 4a delivers the durable, queryable audit record. The increment-3 threat review and decision D-006 expect *monitoring* on top of the log; that layer (forwarding, alerting, retention, tamper-evidence) is a distinct piece of work, recorded here so it is not mistaken for done. |
| D-008 | 2026-05-21 | The per-account verification-code resend cap is enforced best-effort by a count-then-issue check; it is not made fully concurrency-safe. | A plain transaction would not close the check-then-act race (two concurrent resends each reading a count below the cap) without SERIALIZABLE isolation, which is disproportionate for a soft five-per-hour cap. The hard controls are the per-IP rate limiter and the per-code attempt cap; the resend cap is a supplementary account-keyed limit. An exact-concurrent burst may yield a few extra codes — still bounded. |
| D-007 | 2026-05-21 | An unhandled 500 error is logged through Serilog (with its correlation id) but is not written to the operation-log database table. | The operation log is itself a database write; if the database is the failing dependency, auditing a 500 to it would fail too. Security-relevant business events (sign-up, verification, resend, super-admin seed) are written to the operation log by the services; infrastructure failures are captured in the structured application log instead. |
| D-006 | 2026-05-21 | **Accepted risk.** The account-creation endpoints reveal whether an email address is registered — sign-up returns 409 `AUTH_EMAIL_ALREADY_REGISTERED` for an existing address, and verify-email / resend-code return 404 `AUTH_ACCOUNT_NOT_FOUND` for an unknown one. The owner chose to accept and document this rather than make the responses non-revealing. | The increment-3 security review raised account enumeration on these endpoints. The duplicate-email 409 is kept for registration usability. The exposure is accepted on condition of the compensating controls in increment 4 — strict per-account and per-IP rate limiting, and the audit/operation log with monitoring for enumeration patterns. SIMF-API-001 §12.4 and SIMF-FDS-001 §5.1–5.3 already specify this behaviour, so no controlled-document change is needed. Revisit if the threat picture changes. |
| D-005 | 2026-05-21 | ASP.NET Core Identity enforces the SIMF-API-001 §12.5 password baseline (length 8, at least one digit); the request validators add the remaining rules (a letter, not equal to the email). | The increment-3 security review found that leaving Identity's password options permissive meant any credential path not going through the FluentValidation validator — the super-admin seeder, the future password-reset flow — got an effective 1-character policy. §12.5's "enforced in one place" is therefore read as defence-in-depth: the baseline at the Identity credential layer, the full policy with field-level messages in the validators. |
| D-004 | 2026-05-21 | Per-endpoint rate limiting (FastEndpoints `Throttle`) is deferred from increment 3 to increment 4. | FastEndpoints' `Throttle` returns 403 when a request carries no `X-Forwarded-For` header, which breaks direct (non-proxied) callers and the test host. Rate limiting belongs in the increment-4 middleware pipeline (SIMF-Sprint1 plan §7), applied uniformly. |
| D-003 | 2026-05-21 | .NET test assertions use xUnit's built-in `Assert`; FluentAssertions is not adopted. | FluentAssertions v8 carries a commercial-licence model that, combined with the warnings-as-errors build, risks breaking the build. xUnit's own assertions are sufficient and dependency-free. Recorded in SIMF-TST-001 v1.1 §5. |
| D-002 | 2026-05-21 | The solution uses the `.slnx` solution format (`SIMF.slnx`). | The .NET 10 SDK defaults `dotnet new sln` to the new XML solution format. Kept rather than forcing the legacy `.sln` format. |
| D-001 | 2026-05-21 | FastEndpoints registration (`AddFastEndpoints` / `UseFastEndpoints`) is deferred from increment 1 to increment 3. | FastEndpoints throws at startup when no endpoint is declared, and the first endpoint arrives in increment 3 (the authentication endpoints). The `FastEndpoints` package stays referenced by `SIMF.Api`; only the wiring is deferred. |
